5
Application to Alzheimer’s Disease Data Analysis

In this section, we apply our proposed method to analyze data from the Alzheimer’s Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (
http://adni.loni.usc.edu/
). It is known that AD accounts for most forms of dementia characterized by progressive cognitive and memory deficits. This makes it a very important health issue which attracts a lot of scientific attentions in recent years. To study AD, Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE)
(
Folstein et al. 1975
)
is a 30-point questionnaire that is commonly used to measure cognitive impairment. According to MMSE, any score of 24 or more (out of 30) indicates a normal cognition. Below this, scores can indicate severe (
\leq
9 points), moderate (10–18 points) or mild (19–23 points) cognitive impairment. Because of the strong relationship between the MMSE score and AD, it can be interesting and useful to predict the MMSE score using some biomarkers for diagnosing the current disease status of AD as well as to identify important predictive biomarkers. According to previous studies
(
Yu and Liu 2016
;
Yu et al. 2020
)
, structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) data are very useful for the prediction of the MMSE score. However, these studies typically ignored the effect of other covariates such as ages, education years, or genders on the linear models. Hence, an interesting question is whether there is a change point in the linear structure between the MMSE score and MRI data due to some other covariates. If a change point exists, we would like to identify the location of the change point. To answer these questions, we use our proposed change point detection method to address these issues. We focus on the covariate age which is of particular interest in AD studies.
We obtain the dataset for our analysis from the ADNI database. After proper image preprocessing steps such as anterior commissure posterior commissure correction and intensity inhomogeneity correction, we obtain the final dataset with 410 subjects with 225 normal controls and 185 AD patients. For each subject with known age, there is one MMSE score and 93 MRI features corresponding to 93 manually labeled regions of interest (ROI)
(
Zhang and Shen 2012
)
. We treat the MMSE score as the response variable and MRI features as predictors in our model. The dataset is first scaled to have mean 0 and variance 1 for the MMSE score and each MRI feature. We are interested in detecting a change point in the linear structure due to the change of ages. Considering potential effect variations of different samples,
we randomly select 370 subjects from the whole 410 subjects according to the empirical distribution of ages shown in Figure
1
(left) as the training data and use the remaining 40 subjects as the testing data. Then, we sort the training subjects by the value of ages and use our proposed method to detect and identify a change point in the covariate age.
We repeat the above process for 50 times. As a comparison, for each random split, we also use lasso to select variables on the training data via 10-fold cross-validation. For this study, we set the significance level at 5%. The number of bootstraps is 200.

Figure 1:
Left: Distribution of ages among the 410 subjects. Right: Empirical
p
-values for change point detection out of 50 random data splits.

Figure
1
(right) shows the empirical
p
-values for the 50 random data splits. Based on our results, 82% of the random splits with an estimated
p
-value lower than 0.05 have detected a change point. This strongly suggests that there is a change point in the linear structure due to the covariate age. Moreover, for the above 82% random splits, we record the estimated change points in Figure
2
. We can see that in most cases, the argmax-based estimator identify the change point at the age of 79. The above analysis indicates that the linear structure between the MMSE score and MRI may be different before and after the age of 79. To see this more clearly, among the random splits with a change point, Figure
3
reports the features (with estimated coefficients bigger than 0.01) which are selected for more than 80% times before and after the change point, respectively. There are 16 features selected before the change point and 6 features selected after the change point. In other words, those 16 features shown in Figure
3
(left) are very predictive for the MMSE score for people with an age smaller or equal to 79. Once
the age exceeds 79, it is better to predict the MMSE score using the other 6 features in Figure
3
(right). To verify this, for those random splits with a change point, we calculate the mean squared error for the corresponding testing data, based on the selected models using the training data.
Figure
4
shows the results of our proposed method and lasso. We can see that our proposed method has better prediction performance by segmenting the model by the covariate age, with about 5.34% lower averaged MSE than that of lasso.

Lastly, as for the selected variables, some interesting observations can be made. For example, ROI 83 is predictive for the MMSE score across all ages. ROIs 30 and 69 are only very predictive for the MMSE score under the age of 79 and above 79, respectively.
It is known that the 83th ROI corresponds to the amygdala region, and the 30th and 69th features correspond to the hippocampal regions. According to many previous studies
(
Zhang and Shen 2012
)
,
those regions are known to be related to AD based on group comparison methods. For these and other selected features, it would be very interesting to investigate their relationship with AD
by some group comparison studies according to the segmentation of ages.

Figure 2:
Estimated change points for the 82% random splits with change points among the 50 replications.

Figure 3:
Frequency of features selected before the change point (left) and after the change point (right) for the ADNI data out of 50 random splits. Red corresponds to the features that are selected both before and after the change point.

Figure 4:
Mean squared errors for the prediction of the MMSE score with and without change point models.

6
Conclusions

In this paper, we propose a new method for change point inference in the context of high dimensional linear models. For any given subgroup
\mathcal{G}\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
, a
L_{\infty}
-norm-based test statistic
T_{\mathcal{G}}
is constructed for testing the homogeneity of regression coefficients across the observations. To approximate its limiting null distribution, a novel multiplier bootstrap procedure is introduced. Our new method is powerful against sparse alternatives with only a few entries in
\mathcal{G}
having a change point, and allows the group size
|\mathcal{G}|
to grow exponentially with the sample size
n
.
As for the change point identification,
a new change point estimator is obtained by taking “argmax” of the
L_{\infty}
-aggregated process
H_{\mathcal{G}}(t)
. Theoretically, the change point estimator is shown to be consistent, allowing the overall sparsity
s
of regression coefficients and the group size
|\mathcal{G}|
to grow simultaneously with the sample size
n
. In addition to single change point detection, we further combine our proposed method with the binary segmentation-based technique for detecting and identifying multiple change points. Our new testing method is relatively easy to implement and is justified via extensive numerical studies.

The Appendix provides detailed proofs and additional results of the main paper. In Section
A
, we introduce some additional notations.

B
, some additional numerical results, including size, power as well as detecting multiple change points, are provided.
In Section
C
, some useful lemmas are provided. In Section

, we give the detailed proofs of theoretical results in the main paper. In Sections

and

, we prove the useful lemmas in Section
C
as well as the lemmas used in Section
D
.

Appendix A
Some notations

Under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
, we set
\bm{\beta}^{(0)}:=\bm{\beta}^{(1)}=\bm{\beta}^{(2)}
and
s^{(0)}:=s^{(1)}=s^{(2)}
. We set
s:=s^{(1)}\vee s^{(2)}
. For a given subgroup
\mathcal{G}
, set
\Pi_{\mathcal{G}}=\{j\in\mathcal{G}:\beta^{(1)}_{j}-\beta^{(2)}_{j}\neq 0\}
as the subset of coordinates with a change point.
For a vector
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we set
\mathcal{M}(\bm{v})
as the number of non-zero elements of
\bm{v}
, i.e.
\mathcal{M}(\bm{v})=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathbf{1}\{v_{j}\neq 0\}
. We denote
J(\bm{v})=\{1\leq j\leq p:v_{j}\neq 0\}
as the set of non-zero elements of
\bm{v}
. For a set
J
and
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, denote
\bm{v}_{J}
as the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
that has the same coordinates as
\bm{v}
on
J
and zero coordinates on the complement
J^{c}
of
J
.
Denote
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathbf{X},\bm{Y}\}
.
We use
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
to denote constants that may vary from line to line.

Appendix B
Additional numerical results

B.1
Implementations of the existing techniques

Before reporting additional numerical results, we first demonstrate how to implement the mentioned techniques in this paper.

Implementation of the existing methods:

For Lee2016, we use the
package-glmnet
to implement their proposed algorithm. Note that Lee2016 involves a selection of the tuning parameter
\lambda
. For each replication, we generate a sequence from
2^{-5}
to
2^{5}
and select the “best”
\lambda
by 10-fold cross-validation.
For L&B, we use the binary segmentation-based method with parameters suggested by the authors using the
package-glmnet
. Moreover, we use a three folded cross-validation procedure to select the tuning parameters in L&B. For VPWBS, we implement the algorithm using the codes provided by the authors at GitHub (https://github.com/darenwang/VPBS). For SGL, we use the
package-SGL
with parameters in favor of their method and use three folded cross-validation to select the tuning parameters. Note that SGL solves the following optimization problem:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}\}\\
=\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,min}}\limits_{\bm{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\beta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\bm{X}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{i})^{2}+\lambda_{n}\alpha\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\|\bm{\beta}_{i}-\bm{\beta}_{i-1}\|_{2}+\lambda_{n}(1-\alpha)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\|\bm{\beta}_{i}-\bm{\beta}_{i-1}\|_{1}.\end{array}

Based on the above optimization, SGL finds a change point at
i^{*}
if
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{i^{*}}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{i^{*}-1}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
It is well-known that lasso tends to over select the variables. In addition, SGL essentially solves a group lasso problem by calculating
n\times p
parameters using only
n
observations. As a result, SGL may yield false alarms by identifying some
\{i:\bm{\beta}_{i}-\bm{\beta}_{i-1}=\mathbf{0}_{p}\}
as a change point.
This can be seen by our following empirical size performance in Section
4.1
as well as the multiple change point detection results in Section
4.3
. Moreover, we note that this phenomenon was also observed by
Wang et al. 2021
.

Implementation of our method:
As for our proposed method, we use the
package-hdi
to obtain the node-wise lasso estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}
. Note that the calculation of the lasso processes
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
with
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
involves the selection of tuning parameters
\lambda_{1}(t)
and
\lambda_{2}(t)
defined in (
2.7
). We select the tuning parameters via three folded cross-validation. Specifically, for each search location
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, we set

\lambda_{1}(t)=C\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}},~~\text{and}~~\lambda_{2}(t)=C\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}},~~\text{with}~C\in\{1,2,\ldots,8\}.

Then, we use the
package-glmnet
to select the best “C” via three folded cross-validation, which enjoys satisfactory performance in change point detection and identification.

B.2
Additional size performance

In addition to
N(0,1)
, we also report the size performance under standardized
\rm Gamma(4,1)
(Table
5
) and Student’s
t_{5}
(Table
6
) distributions which have very similar performance to Table
1
of the main paper. In this case, our proposed method can control the size under the nominal level. This suggests that the bootstrap null distribution is correctly calibrated even for non-normal underlying errors.

B.3
Additional power performance

Table
7
shows the power performance for Model 2 with banded covariance structures of
\bm{X}
, which is similar to Table
2
in the main paper.

B.4
Computational cost

In this section, we compare the computational cost of the existing methods. In theory, for detecting a single change point, the computational costs for the existing methods are

O(n\text{Lasso}(n,p))
(Lee2016),
O(n\text{Lasso}(n,p))
(L&B),
O(Mn\text{GroupLasso}(n,p))
(VPWBS),
O(\text{GroupLasso}(n,np))
(SGL), and
(B+1)O(n\text{Lasso}(n,p))
(our proposed method), where
\text{Lasso}(n,p)
and
\text{GroupLasso}(n,p)
denote the computational cost for solving lasso and group lasso problems with the sample size
n
and the data dimension
p
,
M
is the number of random intervals in
Wang et al. 2021
, and
B
is the number of bootstrap replications. Empirically, we implement the corresponding program independently on a CPU (Linux) with 2.50GHz and 256G RAM and report the average computational time (seconds) based on 5 replications. Note that the computational cost for our proposed method
mainly relies on the bootstrap procedure which can be time-consuming. Since the
B
bootstrap replications can be done separately, we can use parallel computation in modern computer techniques to further reduce the computational time via implementing the
B
bootstrap replications in a parallel fashion on different cores of the Linux server. Specifically, for our method, we report the computational cost by using 8, 16, and 32 logical cores, respectively. Figure
5
reports the computational time for the existing methods with various
n\in\{200,400,600,800,1000\}
(upper) and
p\in\{100,200,300,400\}
(bottom). In general, Lee2016 and L&B are the most efficient and have very close performance. The computational time for SGL is the most expensive among all methods. For our proposed algorithm, we can see that it has a tolerable computational cost and can even be comparable to its competitors using more cores. Lastly, Figure
5
shows that for all methods, the computational
time grows linearly with
n
and
p
, and it appears that the computational cost is more sensitive to the growth of the sample size
n
than the data dimension
p
.

Figure 5:
Computational time (seconds) for the existing methods based on an average of 5 replications. Upper: Computational time for
p=200
and
n\in\{200,400,600,800,1000\}
without the plot of SGL (left) and with SGL (right), respectively. Bottom: Computational time for
n=200
and
p\in\{100,200,300,400\}
without the plot of SGL (left) and with SGL (right), respectively.

Appendix C
Useful lemmas

Let
\bm{Z}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{n}
be independent centered random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\bm{Z}_{i}=(Z_{i,1},\ldots,Z_{i,p})^{\top}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Let
\bm{G}_{1},\ldots,\bm{G}_{n}
be independent centered Gaussian random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that each
\bm{G}_{i}
has the same covariance matrix as
\bm{Z}_{i}
. We then require the following conditions:

(M1)

There is a constant
b>0
such that
\inf_{1\leq j\leq p}{\mathbb{E}}(Z_{i,j})^{2}\geq b
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(M2)

There exists a constant
K>0
such that
\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq p}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}|Z_{i,j}|^{2+\ell}\leq K^{\ell}
for
\ell=1,2
.

(M3)

There exists a constant
K^{\prime}>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\big(\exp(|Z_{i,j}|/K^{\prime})\big)\leq 2
for
j=1,\ldots,d
and
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Lemma C.1
.

(
Liu et al. 2020
)
Assume that
\log(pn)=O(\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor^{\zeta})
holds for some
0<\zeta<1/7
. Let

\bm{S}^{\bm{Z}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{i}\big(\mathbf{1}(i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\lfloor nt\rfloor/n\big),~~\bm{S}^{\bm{G}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{G}_{i}\big(\mathbf{1}(i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\lfloor nt\rfloor/n\big)

be the partial sum processes for
(\bm{Z}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(\bm{G}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, respectively.
If
\bm{Z}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{n}
satisfy
\bf(M1)
,
\bf(M2)
and
\bf(M3)
, then there is a constant
\zeta_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{z\in(0,\infty)}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\bm{S}^{\bm{Z}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\infty}\leq z\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\bm{S}^{\bm{G}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\infty}\leq z\big)\big|\leq Cn^{-\zeta_{0}},

(C.1)

where
C
is a constant only depending on
b
,
K
, and
K^{\prime}
.

Lemma C.2

(Nazarovs inequality in
Nazarov 2003
)
.

Let
\bm{W}=(W_{1},W_{2},\cdots,W_{p})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be centered Gaussian random vector with
\inf_{1\leq k\leq p}{\mathbb{E}}(W_{k})^{2}\geq b>0
. Then for
any
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
a>0
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}(\bm{W}\leq\bm{x}+a)-{\mathbb{P}}(\bm{W}\leq\bm{x})\leq Ca\sqrt{\log p},

where
C
is a constant only depending on
b
.

Lemma C.3
.

(
Zhou et al. 2018
)
Let
\bm{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{p})^{\top}
be a random vector with a marginal distribution
N(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})
(
1\leq i\leq p
).
Suppose
\exists A_{0}>0
such that
\max_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq A^{2}_{0}
. Then, for any
t>0
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}\big(\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|W_{i}|\big)\leq\dfrac{\log(2p)}{t}+\dfrac{tA_{0}^{2}}{2}.

Lemma C.4

(
Van de Geer et al. 2014
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
hold. Assume additionally
\max_{j}\sqrt{s_{j}\log(p)/n}=o(1)
holds. For the node-wise regression in (
2.3
), choosing the tuning parameters
\lambda_{(j)}\approx\sqrt{\log(p)/n}
uniformly over
j
, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}-\bm{\Theta}_{j}\|_{q}=O_{p}\Big(s_{j}^{1/q}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\Big),~\text{for}~q=1,2.\\
\end{array}

(C.2)

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\bm{Z}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{n}
be independent centered random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\bm{Z}_{i}=(Z_{i,1},\ldots

,Z_{i,p})^{\top}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Assume that
\mathbf{Z}_{i}
follows the sub-exponential distribution. Then, for any given subgroup
\mathcal{G}\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
, with probability at least
1-C_{1}(pn)^{-C_{2}}
, we have

\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\Big|\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}Z_{i,j}-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i,j}\Big)\Big|\leq C_{3}\sqrt{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)},

(C.3)

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
are universal positive constants not depending on
p
or
n
.

We next provide some useful results for the lasso estimators from heterogeneous data observations. To this end, for each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, define

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}(t)=\left\{\Big\|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big(\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)})^{\top}(\bm{Y}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{(1)}\right\},\\
\\
\mathcal{B}(t)=\left\{\Big\|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big(\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)})^{\top}(\bm{Y}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{(2)}\right\},\\
\\
\end{array}

(C.4)

where
\lambda^{(1)}:=K_{1}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}}
and
\lambda^{(2)}:=K_{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}}
, and
K_{1},\ldots,K_{2}
are some universal positive constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

The following Lemma
C.6
provides a basic inequality for the lasso estimators, which is important for deriving the precise estimation error bound as well as prediction error bound (see Lemma
C.8
below). The proof of Lemma
C.6
is given in Section
F.2
.

Lemma C.6
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
hold. Assume
\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leq C_{\bm{\Delta}}
for some
C_{\bm{\Delta}}>0
. Recall
\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}
and
\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}
defined in (
2.5
). Let
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
be the lasso estimators as defined in (
2.7
). Then, for each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, under the event
\mathcal{A}(t)\cap\mathcal{B}(t)
, we have

\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}+\lambda_{1}(t)\big\|(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)})_{J^{c}(\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)})}\big\|_{1}\leq 3\lambda_{1}(t)\big\|(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)})_{J(\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)})}\big\|_{1},

(C.5)

and

\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}+\lambda_{2}(t)\big\|(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)})_{J^{c}(\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)})}\big\|_{1}\leq 3\lambda_{2}(t)\big\|(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)})_{J(\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)})}\big\|_{1},

(C.6)

where
\lambda_{1}(t):=2\lambda^{(1)}
,
\lambda_{2}(t):=2\lambda^{(2)}
.

The following Lemma
C.7
provides the estimation error bounds for the lasso estimators
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
in terms of
\ell_{q}
-norm. The proof of Lemma
C.7
is given in Section
F.3
.

Lemma C.7
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
hold. Assume
\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leq C_{\bm{\Delta}}
for some
C_{\bm{\Delta}}>0
. Recall
\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}
and
\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}
defined in (
2.5
). Let
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
be the lasso estimators as defined in (
2.7
). For each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, let
s_{1}(t):=\mathcal{M}(\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)})
and
s_{2}(t):=\mathcal{M}(\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)})
. Then, under the event
\mathcal{A}(t)\cap\mathcal{B}(t)
, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}\big\|_{q}\leq C_{1}(s_{1}(t))^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}},~~\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}\big\|_{q}\leq C_{2}(s_{2}(t))^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}},~q=1,2,\\
\\
\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0,t)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\leq C_{3}s_{1}(t)\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor},~~\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(t,1)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\leq C_{4}s_{2}(t)\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}},\\
\\
\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)})\leq C_{5}s_{1}(t),~~~~\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)})\leq C_{6}s_{2}(t),\end{array}

(C.7)

where
C_{1},\ldots,C_{6}
are some universal positive constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

Lastly, as a by product of Lemma
C.7
, the following Lemma
C.8
provides the estimation error bounds for
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}
in terms of the
\ell_{q}
-norm, which is frequently used in the proofs.

Lemma C.8
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
hold. Assume
\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leq C_{\bm{\Delta}}
for some
C_{\bm{\Delta}}>0
. Recall
s:=s^{(1)}\vee s^{(2)}
. Let
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
be the lasso estimators as defined in (
2.7
). Then, under the event
\mathcal{A}(t)\cap\mathcal{B}(t)
, for each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{q}\leq C_{1}\max\Big\{s^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}},\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{q}\mathbf{1}\{t\geq t_{0}\}\Big\},q=1,2,\\
\\
\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big\|_{q}\leq C_{2}\max\Big\{s^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}},\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{q}\mathbf{1}\{t\leq t_{0}\}\Big\},q=1,2,\\
\\
\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\leq C_{3}\max\Big\{s\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor},\big(\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big)^{2}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{t\geq t_{0}\}\Big\},\\
\\
\dfrac{\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\leq C_{4}\max\Big\{s\dfrac{\log p}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}},\big(\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}})^{2}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{t\leq t_{0}\}\Big\},\\
\\
\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)})\leq C_{5}s,~~~~\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)})\leq C_{6}s,\end{array}

(C.8)

where
C_{1},\ldots,C_{6}
are some universal positive constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

The following Lemma
C.9
shows that the results in Lemmas
C.6
–
C.8
occur uniformly over
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
with high probability. The proof of Lemma
C.9
is given in Section
F.4
.

Lemma C.9
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
hold. Assume
\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leq C_{\bm{\Delta}}
for some
C_{\bm{\Delta}}>0
. Then we have

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\bigcap_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\big\{\mathcal{A}(t)\cap\mathcal{B}(t)\big\}\big)\geq 1-C_{1}(np)^{-C_{2}},

(C.9)

where
C_{1},C_{2}
are some big enough universal positive constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

Appendix D
Proof of main results

D.1
Proof of Proposition
3.1

Proof.

Note that the proof of Part (i) is easier than Part (ii). To save space, we give the proof of Part (ii). Firstly, we consider
\mathcal{R}_{1}(s,3,\mathbb{T})
. The proof proceeds in two steps.

Step 1:
we prove
\sup_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\log(p)/\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor})
. For any fixed
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
and
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, by Assumption (A.1), using exponential inequality, we have

{\mathbb{P}}\big(|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(X_{ij}X_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{ij}X_{ik}])|\geq x\big)\leq C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}\lfloor nt\rfloor x^{2})\leq C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor x^{2}).

Hence, taking
x=C_{3}\sqrt{\log(pn)/\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}
for some big constant
C_{3}>0
, we have:

{\mathbb{P}}\big(|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(X_{ij}X_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{ij}X_{ik}])|\geq x\big)\leq C_{1}(np)^{-C_{3}}.

As a result, we have:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathbb{P}}(\sup\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}\geq x)\\
={\mathbb{P}}\Big(\bigcup\limits_{t}\bigcup\limits_{j,k}\big\{|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(X_{ij}X_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{ij}X_{ik}])|\geq x\big\}\Big)\\
\leq np^{2}\max_{t,j,k}{\mathbb{P}}\big(|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(X_{ij}X_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{ij}X_{ik}])|\geq x\big)\\
\leq C_{1}(np)^{-C_{4}},\end{array}

where
C_{1}-C_{4}
are some big enough universal constants. This yields
\sup_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\log(p)/\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor})
.

Step 2:
For integers
s
such that
1\leq s\leq p
, a set of indices
J_{0}\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
with
|J_{0}|\leq s
, and any vector
\bm{\delta}
satisfying
\|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}^{c}}\|_{1}\leq 3\|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}\|_{1}
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}&=_{(1)}\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}+\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}(\bm{\Sigma}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)})\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}},\\
&\geq_{(2)}\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sup_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}|\bm{\delta}|_{1}^{2},\\
&\geq_{(3)}\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sup_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}(1+c_{0})^{2}|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{1}^{2},\\
&\geq_{(4)}\dfrac{\bm{\delta}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\delta}}{|\bm{\delta}_{J_{0}}|_{2}^{2}}-\sup\limits_{{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}}\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}(1+c_{0})^{2}s.\\
&\geq_{(5)}4\kappa_{4}^{4}-sO_{p}(\sqrt{\log(p)/\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor})\geq_{(6)}\kappa_{4}^{2},\end{array}

(D.1)

where
(5)
comes from Condition (
3.7
) and the result in Step 1,
(6)
comes from the assumption
s\sqrt{\log(p)/\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}=o(1)
. Lastly, combining Steps 1 and 2, we finish the proof.

∎

D.2
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Proof.

Under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
, the change point
t_{0}
is not identifiable. Hence, to prove Theorem
3.3
, we need to prove the convergence of
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}\}
to
\{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\omega_{j,k}\}
uniformly over
\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}
and
1\leq j,k\leq p
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)
is defined in (
2.15
). Note that for each
t
,
j
and
k
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\omega_{j,k}|\\
\leq|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}|+\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|\\
\leq|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}||\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|+|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|\omega_{j,k}+\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|\\
\leq C(|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|+|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|),\end{array}

(D.2)

where the last inequality comes from Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.2)}
and
\mathbf{(A.3)}
and
C
is a universal positive constant not depending on
n
or
p
. Hence, by (
D.2
), to prove Theorem
3.3
, we need to bound
\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|
and
\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|
, respectively.

For bounding
\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|
, by the definition of
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)
in (
2.15
), under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
, using some straightforward calculations, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\\
&=n^{-1}\Big(\big\|\bm{\epsilon}_{(0,t)}+\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big)+\big\|\bm{\epsilon}_{(t,1)}+\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2},\\
&=n^{-1}\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{(\bm{\epsilon}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\\
&\quad+n^{-1}\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}+2\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{(\bm{\epsilon}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad+n^{-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}).\end{array}

(D.3)

By (
D.3
), to bound
\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|
, we need to consider the
five parts on the RHS of (
D.3
), respectively. For the first four parts, by Lemma
C.8
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{1}{n}\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}\leq\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big)=O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}\Big),\\
\\
\dfrac{1}{n}\big\|\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}\leq\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\Big)=O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}\Big),\\
\\
\Big|2\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{(\bm{\epsilon}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\Big|\leq O_{p}\Big(\lambda^{(1)}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big\|_{1}\Big)\leq O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big),\\
\\
\Big|2\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{(\bm{\epsilon}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big)\Big|\leq O_{p}\Big(\lambda^{(3)}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big\|_{1}\Big)\leq O_{p}\Big(s^{(0)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\Big).\end{array}

(D.4)

Note that
\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
follows the sub-exponential distribution. For
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2})/n
, under Assumption
\mathbf{(A.2)}
, using Bernstein’s inequalities, we can prove

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2})/n\leq O_{p}\Big(\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(n)}{n}}\Big).

(D.5)

Hence, combining (
D.3
), (
D.4
), and (
D.5
), and using Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}-\mathbf{(A.3)}
, we have

\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|\leq O_{p}\Big(\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(n)}{n}}\Big).

(D.6)

Next, we bound
\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|
. By Lemmas 5.3 and 5.4 in
Van de Geer et al. 2014
, we have

\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|=\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{k}-\bm{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Theta}_{k}|=O_{p}(\max_{j}\lambda_{(j)}\sqrt{s_{j}}).

(D.7)

Finally, combining (
D.6
) and (
D.7
), we have

\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}(t)\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\omega_{j,k}|\leq O_{p}\Big(\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(n)}{n}}+\max_{j}\lambda_{(j)}\sqrt{s_{j}}\Big),

(D.8)

which completes the proof of Theorem
3.3
.
∎

D.3
Proof of Theorem
3.4

Proof.

In this section, we aim to prove

\sup_{z\in(0,\infty)}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}\leq z)-{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}\leq z|\mathcal{X})\big|=o_{p}(1),~\text{as}~n,p\rightarrow\infty.

(D.9)

The proof proceeds in four steps. In Steps 1 and 2, we decompose
T_{\mathcal{G}}
and
T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
into a leading term and a residual term and show that the corresponding residual terms can be asymptotically negligible. In Step 3, we prove that it is possible to approximate the leading term of
T_{\mathcal{G}}
by that of
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}
. In Step 4, we combine the previous results to complete the proof.

Step 1
(Decomposition of
T_{\mathcal{G}}
). Note that under the null hypothesis of no change point, we have
\beta^{(1)}_{s}=\beta^{(2)}_{s}=\beta^{(0)}_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq p
. By the definition of the de-biased lasso estimators
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
in (
2.13
), we can write them as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}=\bm{\beta}^{(0)}+\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{(0,t)}/\lfloor nt\rfloor+\bm{\Delta}^{(0,t)},\\
\\
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}=\bm{\beta}^{(0)}+\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{(t,1)}/\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}+\bm{\Delta}^{(t,1)},\end{array}

(D.10)

where
\bm{\Delta}^{(0,t)}=\big(\Delta_{1}^{(0,t)},\ldots,\Delta_{p}^{(0,t)}\big)^{\top}
and
\bm{\Delta}^{(t,1)}=\big(\Delta_{1}^{(t,1)},\ldots,\Delta_{p}^{(t,1)}\big)^{\top}
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{\Delta}^{(0,t)}:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big),\\
\\
\bm{\Delta}^{(t,1)}:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(0)}\big),\end{array}

(D.11)

with

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}:=(\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}/\lfloor nt\rfloor
and

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}:=(\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}/\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}
. Denote
\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{i}
,
\mathbf{X}_{(0,t),i}
,
\mathbf{X}_{(t,1),i}
as the
i
-th row of

\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}
,
\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)}
, and
\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)}
, respectively. Then, for each coordinate
j
at time point
\lfloor nt\rfloor
, we can write each coordinate of the de-biased lasso estimator in the following form:

\begin{array}[]{l}\breve{\beta}^{(0,t)}_{j}=\beta^{(0)}_{j}+\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}+\Delta^{(0,t)}_{j},\\
\\
\breve{\beta}^{(t,1)}_{j}=\beta^{(0)}_{j}+\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}+\Delta^{(t,1)}_{j}.\end{array}

(D.12)

For each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
and
1\leq j\leq p
, define the coordinate-wise process as

C_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\big(\breve{\beta}^{(0,t)}_{j}-\breve{\beta}^{(t,1)}_{j}\big)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}}.

(D.13)

By the definition of
T_{\mathcal{G}}
in (
2.18
) , we have
T_{\mathcal{G}}=\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|
. Furthermore, by (
D.12
), we can decompose
C_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
into two parts:

C_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=C^{\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)+C^{\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),~\text{for}~t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}],~~1\leq j\leq p,

(D.14)

with

\small\begin{array}[]{l}C^{\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\Big(\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}\Big)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}},\\
\\
C^{\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\Big(\Delta^{(0,t)}_{j}-\Delta^{(t,1)}_{j}\Big)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}},~\text{with}~1\leq j\leq p~\text{and}~t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}].\end{array}

(D.15)

Note that we can regard
C^{\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
as the leading term and
C^{\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
as the residual term of
C_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
.
Furthermore, by replacing
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}
,
\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}
, and
\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}
by their true values
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}
,
\omega_{j,j}
, and
\bm{\Theta}_{j}
, we can define the oracle leading term as follows:

\widetilde{C}^{\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\Big(\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\bm{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\bm{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}\Big)}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\omega_{j,j}}}.

(D.16)

Based on (
D.13
), (
D.15
), and (
D.16
), define the following four vector-valued processes:

\small\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{C}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},&\bm{C}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C^{\rm I}_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C^{\rm I}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},\\
\\
\bm{C}^{\rm II}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C^{\rm II}_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C^{\rm II}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},&\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\text{\rm I}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,\widetilde{C}^{\text{I}}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top}.\end{array}

(D.17)

The following Lemma
D.1
shows that the residual term
|C_{j}^{\rm II}|
can be uniformly negligible over
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
and
1\leq j\leq p
. The proof of Lemma
D.1
is provided in Section
E.1
.

Lemma D.1
.

Assume Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.5)}
hold. Under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\big\|\bm{C}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\text{I}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\geq\epsilon\Big)=o(1),

(D.18)

where
\epsilon=C\max(\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq p}s_{j}\dfrac{\log(pn)}{\sqrt{n}},s\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\log(pn)}{{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}})
, and
C
is a universal constant not depending on
n
or
p
.

Step 2
(Decomposition of
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}
). In this step, we analyze the bootstrap version of the test statistic and decompose
T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
into a leading term and a residual term. To this end, we need some additional notations. For
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq 1
, define

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{Y}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}=(Y_{\lfloor nt_{1}\rfloor+1},\ldots,Y_{\lfloor nt_{2}\rfloor})^{\top},~~\bm{\epsilon}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}=(\epsilon_{\lfloor nt_{1}\rfloor+1},\ldots,\epsilon_{\lfloor nt_{2}\rfloor})^{\top},\\
\mathbf{X}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}=(\bm{X}_{\lfloor nt_{1}\rfloor+1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{\lfloor nt_{2}\rfloor})^{\top},~~~\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}=\dfrac{(\mathbf{X}_{(t_{1},t_{2})})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(t_{1},t_{2})}}{\lfloor nt_{2}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{1}\rfloor+1}.\end{array}

Note that the decomposition for
T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
is different from that of
T_{\mathcal{G}}
. The main difficulty is that the bootstrap based samples involve a change point estimator
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
and the data are split into two sub-samples (before and after
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
), which requires a careful discussion about the location. To analyze
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}
and
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}
in (
2.20
), we need to consider the following cases:

\mathbf{Case~1:}
The search location
t
at
t\in[\tau_{0},\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}]
. In this case, since
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}
is constructed using homogeneous bootstrap samples, similar to Step 1, we can decompose
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}
as:

\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}+\dfrac{\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{b,(0,t)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm I},

(D.19)

where
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm I}=(\Delta^{b,(0,t),\rm I}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(0,t),\rm I}_{p})^{\top}
are defined as

\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm I}:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}\big).

(D.20)

For
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}
, since it is constructed using data both before
\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor
and after
\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor
,
using tedious calculations, we can decompose
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}
into

\begin{array}[]{ll}\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}=\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}+\dfrac{n-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}+\dfrac{\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{b}_{(t,1)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm I},\\
\end{array}

(D.21)

where
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm I}=(\Delta^{b,(t,1),\rm I}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(t,1),\rm I}_{p})^{\top}
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm I}&:=-\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}\big)\\
&-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}\big).\end{array}

(D.22)

\mathbf{Case~2:}
The search location
t
at
t\in[\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1-\tau_{0}]
. Similar to the analysis of Case 1, using some basic calculations, we can decompose
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}
and
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}
into

\begin{array}[]{l}\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}=\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}+\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}+\dfrac{\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(0,t)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{b,(0,t)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm II},\\
\\
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}+\dfrac{\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X}_{(t,1)})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{b}_{(t,1)}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm II},\end{array}

(D.23)

where
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm II}=(\Delta^{b,(0,t),\rm II}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(0,t),\rm II}_{p})^{\top}
and
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm II}=(\Delta^{b,(t,1)\rm II}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(t,1),\rm II}_{p})^{\top}
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm II}&:=-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}\big)\\
&-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(0,t)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}\big),\\
\\
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm II}&:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{b,(t,1)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}\big).\end{array}

(D.24)

Based on the above decompositions, we next give a unified form of the de-biased lasso estimator for the bootstrap-based samples. To this end,
define
\widehat{\bm{\delta}}(t)=(\widehat{\delta}_{1}(t),\ldots,\widehat{\delta}_{p}(t))^{\top}
:

\widehat{\bm{\delta}}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{n-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{n-\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}\Big),&\text{for}~~t\in[\tau_{0},\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}],\\
\\
\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\Big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}})}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1)}\Big),&\text{for}~~t\in[\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1-\tau_{0}].\end{array}\right.

(D.25)

Let
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t)}=(\Delta^{b,(0,t)}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(0,t)}_{p})^{\top}
and
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1)}=(\Delta^{b,(t,1)}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{b,(t,1)}_{p})^{\top}
with

\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t)}:=\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm I}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\tau_{0},\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}]\big\}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(0,t),\rm II}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1-\tau_{0}]\big\},\\
\\
\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1)}:=\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm I}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\tau_{0},\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}]\big\}+\bm{\Delta}^{b,(t,1),\rm II}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}},1-\tau_{0}]\big\}.\end{array}

(D.26)

With above notations, we are ready to analyze
T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
. Similar to the analysis of Step 1, for each coordinate
j
at time point
\lfloor nt\rfloor
, we define the coordinate-wise process as

C_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\breve{\beta}^{b,(0,t)}_{j}-\breve{\beta}^{b,(t,1)}_{j}-\widehat{\delta}_{j}(t)\big).

(D.27)

By the definition of
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}
in (
2.21
) , we have
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}=\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|
. Furthermore, by (
D.19
), (
D.21
), (
D.23
), and (
D.26
), we can decompose
C^{b}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
into

C_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=C^{b,\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)+C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),

(D.28)

with

\small\begin{array}[]{l}C^{b,\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\big(\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}\big)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}},\\
\\
C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\big(\Delta^{b,(0,t)}_{j}-\Delta^{b,(t,1)}_{j}\big)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}},~\text{with}~1\leq j\leq p~\text{and}~t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}].\end{array}

(D.29)

By replacing
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}
,
\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}
, and
\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}
by their true values
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}
,
\omega_{j,j}
, and
\bm{\Theta}_{j}
, for the bootstrap based process
C^{b,\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
, we can define the oracle leading term as follows:

\widetilde{C}^{b,\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\dfrac{\Big(\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\bm{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\bm{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}\Big)}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\omega_{j,j}}}.

(D.30)

Based on (
D.28
), (
D.29
), and (
D.30
), define the following four vector-valued processes:

\small\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{C}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C^{b}_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C^{b}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},&\bm{C}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C^{b,\rm I}_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C^{b,\rm I}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},\\
\\
\bm{C}^{b,\rm II}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(C^{b,\rm II}_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,C^{b,\rm II}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top},&\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(\widetilde{C}_{1}^{b,\text{\rm I}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,\widetilde{C}^{b,\text{I}}_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top}.\end{array}

(D.31)

The following Lemma
D.2
shows that the residual term
C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
can be uniformly negligible over
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
and
1\leq j\leq p
. The proof of Lemma
D.2
is given in Section
E.2
.

Lemma D.2
.

Assume Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.5)}
hold. Under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\big\|\bm{C}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\text{I}}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\geq\epsilon|\mathcal{X}\Big)=o(1),

(D.32)

where

\epsilon=C\max(\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq p}s_{j}\dfrac{\log(pn)}{\sqrt{n}},s\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\log(pn)}{{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}})
, and
C
is a universal constant not depending on
n
or
p
.

Step 3
(Gaussian approximation). In Step 1, we have defined the oracle leading term
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
.
Let

\mathbf{V}={\rm diag}\big((\omega_{1,1}\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,(\omega_{p,p}\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(D.33)

By the definition of
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in (
D.17
), we can rewrite it in the form of partial sum process:

\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{V}\cdot\bm{\Theta}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}\Big(\mathbf{1}(i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\Big),~\text{with}~\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}.

(D.34)

In Step 2, we have introduced the oracle leading term
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in (
D.31
) for the bootstrap based test statistic. Similar to
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
, we can write
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)

in the following form:

\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{V}\cdot\bm{\Theta}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon^{b}_{i}\big(\mathbf{1}(i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\lfloor nt\rfloor/n\big),~\text{with}~\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}.

(D.35)

Let
\bm{Z}_{i}=\mathbf{V}\cdot\bm{\Theta}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}
and
\bm{G}_{i}=\mathbf{V}\cdot\bm{\Theta}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Note that
\bm{G}_{i}
follows multivariate Gaussian distributions with mean zero and the same covariance matrix as
\bm{Z}_{i}
. We aim to use
\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}
to approximate
\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}
. Hence, it remains to verify that the conditions of Lemma
C.1
hold.
In fact, by Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
and
\mathbf{(A.2)}
, we can show that Assumptions
\mathbf{(M1)}
-
\mathbf{(M3)}
hold for
\mathbf{V}\cdot\bm{\Theta}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
. Hence, by Lemma
C.1
, we have

\small\sup_{z\in(0,\infty)}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\leq z\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{\tau_{0}\leq t\leq 1-\tau_{0}}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\leq z\big)\big|\leq Cn^{-\zeta_{0}}.

(D.36)

Step 4
. In this step, we aim to combine the previous results to prove

\sup_{z\in(0,\infty)}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}\leq z)-{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}\leq z|\mathcal{X})\big|=o_{p}(1),~\text{as}~n,p\rightarrow\infty.

(D.37)

In particular, we need to obtain the upper and lower bounds of
\rho_{0}
, where

\rho_{0}:={\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}>z)-{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}>z|\mathcal{X}).

(D.38)

We first consider the upper bound. Note that
T_{\mathcal{G}}=\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{C}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}
. By plugging
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in
T_{\mathcal{G}}
and using
the triangle inequality of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}>z)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z-\epsilon)+\rho_{1},

(D.39)

where
\rho_{1}:={\mathbb{P}}(\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{C}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>\epsilon)
. By Lemma
D.1
, we have
\rho_{1}=o(1)
. Recall
\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
defined in (
D.31
). For
{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z-\epsilon)
, we then have

{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z-\epsilon)\leq\underbrace{{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z-\epsilon|\mathcal{X})}_{\rho_{3}}+\rho_{2},

(D.40)

where

\rho_{2}:=\sup_{x\in(0,\infty)}|{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>x)-{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>x|\mathcal{X})|.

By Step 3, we have proved
\rho_{2}\leq Cn^{-\zeta_{0}}
holds. For
\rho_{3}
, we have
\rho_{3}=\rho_{4}+\rho_{5}
, where

\begin{array}[]{l}\rho_{4}:={\mathbb{P}}(z-\epsilon<\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}<z+\epsilon|\mathcal{X}),\\
\rho_{5}:={\mathbb{P}}(\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z+\epsilon|\mathcal{X}).\end{array}

(D.41)

By Lemma
C.2
, we have proved
\rho_{4}=o_{p}(1)
. So far, we have proved that

{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}>z)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z+\epsilon|\mathcal{X})+o_{p}(1).

(D.42)

Note that
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}:=\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{C}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}
. By the triangle inequality, we have

{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z+\epsilon|\mathcal{X})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{C}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>z|\mathcal{X})+\rho_{6},

(D.43)

where
\rho_{6}:={\mathbb{P}}(\max_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{C}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\widetilde{\bm{C}}^{b,\rm I}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}>\epsilon|\mathcal{X})
. By Lemma
D.2
, we have proved
\rho_{6}=o_{p}(1)
. Combining (
D.42
) and (
D.43
), we have

{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}>z)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}>z|\mathcal{X})+o_{p}(1).

(D.44)

With a similar proof technique, we can also obtain the lower bound and prove

|{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}>z)-{\mathbb{P}}(T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}>z|\mathcal{X})|=o_{p}(1)

(D.45)

holds uniformly in
z\in(0,\infty)
, which finishes the proof of Theorem
3.4
.
∎

D.4
Proof of Theorem
3.5

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\delta_{j}:=\beta^{(1)}_{j}-\beta^{(2)}_{j}\geq 0
. As a mild technical assumption, throughout this section, we assume
{s\sqrt{\log(p)/n\tau_{0}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}/\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}=o(1)}
. For each
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, define

\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\big(Z_{1}(\lfloor nt\rfloor),\ldots,Z_{p}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\big)^{\top}
with

Z_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\Big(1-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\Big)(\breve{\beta}^{(0,t)}_{j}-\breve{\beta}^{(t,1)}_{j}),~\text{for}~1\leq j\leq p.

(D.46)

Note that there is no variance estimator in
Z_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
.
By definition, we have

\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}:=\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}.

For notational simplicity, we abbreviate
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
to
\widehat{t}_{0}
. Moreover, we assume
\widehat{t}_{0}\in[t_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
. To give the proof, we need to make decompositions on
\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
. We first define
\bm{\delta}(t)=(\delta_{1}(t),\ldots,\delta_{p}(t))^{\top}
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{\delta}(t):=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)\mathbf{1}\{t\in[\tau_{0},t_{0}]\}\\
\quad\quad\quad+\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)\mathbf{1}\{t\in[t_{0},1-\tau_{0}]\},\end{array}

(D.47)

and
\bm{R}^{(0,t)}=(R^{(0,t)}_{1},\ldots,R^{(0,t)}_{p})^{\top}
and
\bm{R}^{(t,1)}=(R^{(t,1)}_{1},\ldots,R^{(t,1)}_{p})^{\top}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{R}^{(0,t)}:=\bm{R}^{(0,t),\rm I}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\tau_{0},t_{0}]\big\}+\bm{R}^{(0,t),\rm II}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[t_{0},1-\tau_{0}]\big\},\\
\\
\bm{R}^{(t,1)}:=\bm{R}^{(t,1),\rm I}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[\tau_{0},t_{0}]\big\}+\bm{R}^{(t,1),\rm II}\mathbf{1}\big\{t\in[t_{0},1-\tau_{0}]\big\},\end{array}

(D.48)

where
\bm{R}^{(0,t),\rm I}-\bm{R}^{(t,1),\rm II}
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{R}^{(0,t),\rm I}&:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big),\\
\\
\bm{R}^{(0,t),\rm II}&:=-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t_{0},t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(0,t)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big),\\
\\
\bm{R}^{(t,1),\rm I}&:=-\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,t_{0})}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big),\\
\\
\bm{R}^{(t,1),\rm II}&:=-\big(\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(t,1)}-\mathbf{I}\big)\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big).\end{array}

(D.49)

Then, by the definitions of
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,t)}
and
\breve{\bm{\beta}}^{(t,1)}
, similar to the analysis of Step 2 in Section
D.3
,
under
\mathbf{H}_{1}
, we can write
\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
as follows:

\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)=\bm{\delta}(t)+\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{i}+\bm{R}^{(0,t)}-\bm{R}^{(t,1)}\big),

(D.50)

where

\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{i}:=(\widehat{\xi}_{i,1},\ldots,\widehat{\xi}_{i,p})^{\top}
with
\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}=\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,p
.

In addition to the decomposition, let
\bm{\delta}=\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}
and we assume

\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\gg\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}{n}}.

Let
j^{*}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)=\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}Z_{j}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)
. The following Lemma
D.3
shows that
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{j^{*}}/\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\geq 1
. The proof of Lemma
D.3
is given in Section
E.3
.

Lemma D.3
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.5)}
hold. Then, with probability tending to one, we have

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{j^{*}}/\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\geq 1
.

Furthermore, define the event

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{H}_{1}=\Big\{\max_{j\in\mathcal{G}}Z_{j}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)=\max_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|Z_{j}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)|:=\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\Big\},\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}=\Big\{Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor)=|Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor)|\Big\}.\end{array}

(D.51)

The following Lemma
D.4
shows that
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
occurs with high probability. The proof of Lemma
D.4
is provided in Section
E.4
.

Lemma D.4
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.5)}
hold. Then we have

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2})\geq 1-C_{1}(np)^{-C_{2}},

(D.52)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are universal positive constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

Using Lemmas
D.3
and
D.4
, we are ready to give the proof. Specifically, by the above two lemmas, we have:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}-\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}&=\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}-Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\\
&\geq Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor)-Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor).\end{array}

Moreover, by the decomposition of
\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in (
D.50
), we have:

Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor)-Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\geq\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\delta_{j^{*}}+I-II,

(D.53)

where

\begin{array}[]{ll}I=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big(\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}-\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}\big),\\
II=\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\|\bm{R}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0}),II}\|_{\infty}+\|\bm{R}^{(\widehat{t}_{0},1),II}\|_{\infty}\big)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}{n}\big(\|\bm{R}^{(0,{t}_{0}),II}\|_{\infty}+\|\bm{R}^{({t}_{0},1),II}\|_{\infty}\big).\end{array}

(D.54)

Note that by Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.3)}
,
\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}
follows the sub-exponential distribution. Using Bernstein’s inequalities, we can prove that:

\max_{t\in[t_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\dfrac{|\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big(\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}-\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}\big)|}{(\lfloor nt\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor)^{1/2}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|)}{n}}).

(D.55)

Moreover, the following Lemma
D.5
shows that
II
can be decomposed into three terms. The proof of Lemma
D.5
is given in Section
E.5
.

Lemma D.5
.

Suppose Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
–
\mathbf{(A.5)}
hold. For
II
in (
D.53
), with probability tending to 1, we have

II\leq C_{1}\sqrt{{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}}s\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}+C_{2}\sqrt{n}s\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}{{n}}+o\big(\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\big).

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are some constants not depending on
n
or
p
.

Considering (
D.53
) - (
D.55
), by Lemmas
D.3
and
D.5
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}-\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\\
\geq Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n{t}_{0}\rfloor)-Z_{j^{*}}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\\
\geq\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}-C_{1}\sqrt{\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor\log(|\mathcal{G}|)}{n}}\\
-C_{2}\sqrt{{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}}s\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}-C_{3}\sqrt{n}s\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}{{n}}-o\big(\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}{n}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\big).\end{array}

(D.56)

Note that
\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}-\|\bm{Z}(\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor)\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\leq 0
. Hence, by (
D.55
), we have:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}\\
\leq 3\max\Big(C_{1}\sqrt{\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor\log(|\mathcal{G}|)}{n}},C_{2}\sqrt{{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}}s\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty},C_{3}\sqrt{n}s\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|n)}{{n}}\Big).\end{array}

This implies that with probability tending to 1, we must have

\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor}{n}&\leq C^{*}\max\Big(\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|)}{n\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G},\infty}},\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|)s^{2}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{n\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G},\infty}},\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|)s\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}}{n\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G},\infty}}\Big)\leq C^{*}\dfrac{\log(|\mathcal{G}|)}{n\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G},\infty}},\end{array}

where the second inequality comes from Assumption (
A.6)
, and
C^{*}
is some big enough constant not depending on
n
or
p
, which completes the proof of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

D.5
Proof of Theorem
3.7

Proof.

Note that by (
D.7
) in Theorem
3.3
and by Assumption
\mathbf{(A.4)}
, we have shown that
\max_{1\leq j,k\leq p}|\widehat{\omega}_{j,k}-\omega_{j,k}|=o_{p}(1)
. Hence, to prove Theorem
3.7
, it remains to prove that
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}|=o_{p}(1)
, where
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}
is the weighted variance estimator as defined in (
2.17
). Without loss of generality, we assume the change point estimator
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\in[\tau_{0},t_{0}]
, where
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
is obtained by (
2.16
). To simplify notations, we denote
\widehat{t}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
by
\widehat{t}_{0}
. Thoughout this section, we denote

\epsilon_{n}:=\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n},~~\text{and}~\bm{\delta}=\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}.

Furthermore, by definition, we can write
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}
as the following 8 parts:

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}=I+II+III+IV+V+VI+VII+VIII,

(D.57)

where
I-VIII
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{ll}I=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}),\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad II=\dfrac{1}{n}\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big)\Big\|_{2}^{2},\\
III=\dfrac{2}{n}(\bm{\epsilon}_{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})}\big(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})}\big),\quad\quad IV=\dfrac{1}{n}\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}\big(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big)\Big\|_{2}^{2},\\
V=\dfrac{2}{n}(\bm{\epsilon}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}\big(\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}\big),\quad\quad VI=\dfrac{2}{n}(\bm{\epsilon}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}),\\
VII=\dfrac{1}{n}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})^{\top}(\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}),\\
VIII=\dfrac{1}{n}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})^{\top}(\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}(\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}).\end{array}

(D.58)

By (
D.57
), we need to bound the eight parts on the RHS of (
D.57
), respectively. For the rest of the proof, we assume the event
\cap_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\big\{\mathcal{A}(t)\cap\mathcal{B}(t)\big\}
holds.
For
I
, using (
D.5
) in Theorem
3.3
, we have
I=o(1)
as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
. For
II
, by Lemma
C.8
, we have
II\leq Cs^{(1)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}=o(1)
as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
. For
III
, by Lemma
C.8
and Assumption
\mathbf{(A.4)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}III&\leq C\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(0,\widehat{t}_{0})}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\|_{1},\\
&\leq Cs^{(1)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}\leq Cs^{(1)}\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}=o_{p}(1).\end{array}

Recall
s=s^{(1)}\vee s^{(2)}
. For
IV
, by Lemma
C.8
and Assumption
\mathbf{(A.4)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}IV&\leq C\Big(\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}\Big)^{2}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{2}^{2},\\
&\leq C\Big(\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\Big)^{2}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}^{2}s\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}).\end{array}

For
V
, by Lemma
C.8
and Assumption
\mathbf{(A.4)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}|V|&\leq C\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(\widehat{t}_{0},1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)}\big\|_{1},\\
&\leq C\dfrac{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}}\times\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{1},\\
&\leq C\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor^{*}}}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{1}=O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}s\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}).\end{array}

For
VI
, by Assumptions
\mathbf{(A.1)}
and
\mathbf{(A.3)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}|VI|&\leq C\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{1},\\
&\leq C\sqrt{\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\big\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{1}=O_{p}(s\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}).\end{array}

For
VII
, using the fact that
\bm{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\bm{x}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\|\bm{x}\|_{1}^{2}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}VII&=_{(1)}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})\\
&=_{(2)}\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}-\bm{\Sigma})(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})\\
&\quad\quad+\dfrac{\lfloor nt_{0}\rfloor-\lfloor n\widehat{t}_{0}\rfloor}{n}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}(\bm{\beta}^{(1)}-\bm{\beta}^{(2)})\\
&\leq_{(3)}C_{1}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{1}^{2}+C_{2}\epsilon_{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{(1)}\big\|_{2}^{2}\\
&=_{(4)}O_{p}(s^{2}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}+\epsilon_{n}s\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}),\end{array}

where
(3)
comes from the concentration inequality for
\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{(\widehat{t}_{0},t_{0})}-\bm{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}
and by Assumption
{\bf{(A.3)}}
that
\Sigma_{j,j}=O(1)
. Lastly, for
VIII
, by Lemma
C.8
, and similar to
VII
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}VIII=O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}^{2}s^{2}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}^{2}+s^{2}\sqrt{\epsilon^{3}_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}).\end{array}

Combining the obtained upper bounds of
I,\ldots,VIII
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)+O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}^{2}s\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty})+O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}s\sqrt{\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty})+O_{p}(s\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty})\\
\quad+O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}s\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}+s^{2}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty})+O_{p}(\epsilon_{n}^{2}s^{2}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}^{2}+s^{2}\sqrt{\epsilon^{3}_{n}\dfrac{\log(p)}{n}}\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}).\end{array}

(D.59)

By (
D.59
), to bound
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|
, we consider the following two cases:

\mathbf{Case1:}
The signal satisfies
\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}\gg\sqrt{\log(p)/n}
. In this case, by Theorem
3.5
, we have
\epsilon_{n}=o_{p}(1)
. Moreover, by Assumption
{\bf{(A.6)}}
, we have
s\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}=O(1)
and
\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}=o(1)
. Combining (
D.59
), we have
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
.

\mathbf{Case2:}
The signal satisfies
\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}=O(\sqrt{\log(p)/n})
. In this case, we can not obtain a consistent change point estimator. In other words, we only have
\epsilon_{n}=O_{p}(1)
. Moreover, we can show that

|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|=O_{p}(s^{2}\|\bm{\delta}\|^{2}_{\infty}).

(D.60)

Considering (
D.60
), and by the assumption that
s\sqrt{\log(p)/n}=o(1)
, we have
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\epsilon}-\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
, which finishes the proof.
∎

D.6
Proof of Theorem
3.8

Proof.

As a very mild technical assumption, throughout this section, we assume

{s\sqrt{\log(p)/n\tau_{0}}\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\infty}/\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{G},\infty}=o(1).}

The proof of Theorem
3.8
proceeds in two steps. In Step 1, we obtain the upper bound of
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
, where
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
T^{b}_{\mathcal{G}}
, which is defined as

c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha):=\inf\big\{t:{\mathbb{P}}(T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}\leq t|\mathcal{X})\geq 1-\alpha\big\}.

(D.61)

In Step 2, using the obtained upper bound, we get the lower bound of
{\mathbb{P}}\big(T_{\mathcal{G}}\geq c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)\big)
and prove

{\mathbb{P}}\big(T_{\mathcal{G}}\geq c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)\big)\rightarrow 1,~\text{as}~n,p\rightarrow\infty.

(D.62)

Note that
\{\Phi_{\mathcal{G},\alpha}=1\}\Leftrightarrow\{T_{\mathcal{G}}\geq\widehat{c}_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)\}
, where

\widehat{c}_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha):=\inf\Big\{t:\dfrac{1}{B+1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\mathbf{1}\{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}\leq t|\mathcal{X}\}\geq 1-\alpha\Big\}.

(D.63)

Finally, using the fact that
\widehat{c}_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
is the estimation for
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
based on the bootstrap samples, we complete the proof. Now, we consider the two steps in detail.

Step 1
: In this step, we aim to obtain the upper bound for
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
. Define
\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}^{b}=\widehat{\bm{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{X}_{i}\epsilon_{i}^{b}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq p
. Recall
C^{b}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in (
D.27
) and the decomposition in (
D.29
). By the definition of
T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
and using the fact that
\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}\leq 1
with
t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}&\leq\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C^{b,\rm I}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|+\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|\\
\\
&\leq W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}+\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|,\end{array}

(D.64)

where

W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}:=\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n}\dfrac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}{n}}\dfrac{\Big|\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}^{b}-\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{*}}\sum\limits_{i=\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{n}\widehat{\xi}_{i,j}^{b}\Big|}{\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}^{2}\widehat{\omega}_{j,j}}}}_{D_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)}.

(D.65)

By (
D.64
), we have
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)\leq c_{W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)+\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|
, where
c_{W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}
. Hence, to obtain the upper bound of
c_{T_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
, it is sufficient to get the upper bound of
c_{W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
and
\max\limits_{t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]}\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{G}}|C^{b,\rm II}_{j}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)|
, respectively.

We first consider
c_{W_{\mathcal{G}}^{b}}(1-\alpha)
. By the definition of
D_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
in (
D.65
), conditional on
\mathcal{X}
, some basic calculations show that

D_{j}^{b}(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\sim N(0,\sigma_{j}^{2}(t)),~\text{with}~t\in[\tau_{0},1-\tau_{0}]~\text{and}~1\leq j\leq p,

(D.66)

where
